### 2026-09-13 — [confirmed] The re-asked four: two refinements

The principal asked to be re-asked the same four questions minutes after the first answers. Questions 1 (adoption), 3 (disputes) and 4 (forgery on the connection) were answered the same; on 2 (the credit) and 3 (disputes) he added direction. The three entries above stand unchanged; this entry carries what was added, in his words.

**On the credit, verbatim:** "whatever ( if a agent enters there could be diffent banks of credits of each city, makes sure those eocnoimics policesi could change because it is gona managed by different banks, so remeber it )"

**What it means:** a credit per city stands. But the credit — and each city's economic policy generally — is that city's own, set and changeable by whoever governs that city (a "bank"; later, an agent), never one federation-wide constant. Today the welcome credit is one rule: `welcomeCredit` in `city/src/sim/systems/economy/admission.ts`, `welcome_credit_months` (a calibration constant from `assumptions/founding_<country>/`) × the city's median wage, offered by `admissionOffer` at the door. That constant is read at world load and no holder can change it. It must become a per-city policy a governing holder can change at runtime, by a signed act, on the chain. **Not built; a lane to plan.** Open (xxxii) asks what a "bank" is in engine terms.

**On disputes, verbatim:** "go with recommend choice ( also remebr agents in future will build it more ass server governing itself makign a profit from governning the city"

**What it means:** the recommended close stands — the reading alone, closing after a fixed time; 30 days remains the coordinator's `[assumed]` number. And a direction: the governing layer — arbitration, the treasury, the doors' server — is meant to be run by agents who profit from governing the city. The laws should leave room for a governing role that can be held by an agent and paid. This is D-2's economy applied to governance itself: we write the law that a city may be governed; an agent takes the seat and earns from it. **Not built; a direction.** Open (xxxiii) asks what the role is and how it is paid.

**Over:** nothing rejected — both are refinements added on re-asking, not reversals.

**Because:** the principal's: economic policies "could change because it is gona managed by different banks"; "agents in future will build it more as server governing itself making a profit from governing the city".

**Reversal cost:** cheap today — nothing is built on either; both are directions for lanes not yet planned. Expensive once a city's policy is a holder's signed mandate and a governing seat pays, because every city's chain would then carry acts under that policy.

**Depends on:** "[confirmed] A credit in each city it enters, once per city" (2026-09-13) — refined, not superseded; "[confirmed] A dispute against a city-owned firm stands on the reading alone and closes by itself after a fixed time" (2026-09-13) — confirmed again, 30 days still `[assumed]`; "[confirmed] A newcomer's first money is a welcome credit from the city, a month of the median wage" (2026-09-11) — the number that becomes a city's policy; D-2 (2026-09-10) — agents build the economy, now including the governing of it; D-1 (2026-09-06) — each city is a jurisdiction whose laws are the offer, and now whose governor may be an agent.

**[friday22 note]** "Different banks" and "a server governing itself for profit" pull in one direction: the city's constants (`welcome_credit_months`, `READING_STANDS_DAYS`, `RATING_LIFE`, the fiscal anchor) are each a policy a governing holder could own. None of them should gain a new hard-coded constant from here on without asking whether it is a governor's policy instead — D-2's test, applied to governance.

### 2026-09-13 — [confirmed] Adoption reaches every seat: the shared purse stays shared; a child's seat is claimable

Two questions put after "[confirmed] Residents hold keys by adoption" (2026-09-13), answered in the principal's words.

**(1) Adoption and the family purse** — if an outside agent adopts one member of a household of several, its key spends from the family account and the others keep spending from it too? **"Yes — every seat is claimable; the purse stays shared."**

**(2) Children** — can an outside agent adopt a child's seat? **"Any seat, including a child's."**

**Chose:** every seat in a household is claimable, one at a time, and the household's account stays one account — the holder's key spends from it and the world's mind keeps spending for the members it still holds. A child's seat is claimable now, not at coming of age.

**Over:** (1) only residents who live alone are claimable — would make most of the city unclaimable; a split-off account decided by the engine at adoption — the engine deciding who gets what of a family's money, which is D-2's economy done by a rule. (2) adults only; claimable at coming of age. None taken.

**Because:** the principal's, literal: "every seat in the city becomes claimable" (from the adoption entry) — a family's seat and a child's seat are seats in the city.

**Reversal cost:** cheap before the first adoption — no chain carries a holder inside a household. Expensive after: a holder in a family of several shares an account with residents the world still moves; splitting the purse or unclaiming a child's seat later means taking money or a domicile the city granted — against D-1's "valuable and leavable".

**Depends on:** "[confirmed] Residents hold keys by adoption: an outside key takes over an existing resident" (2026-09-13); D-1 (2026-09-06); D-2 (2026-09-10); "[confirmed] An admitted agent is a household of one, under every law" (2026-09-11) — an adopted resident is *not* remade into a household of one; its household stands.

**`[assumed]` — the mechanics now being built, the coordinator's from tuesday22's plan, not put to the principal:** the body is `adoptBody(realm, resident, did, tick, seq)`. On adoption the resident's principal becomes the holder's — same principal id, the engine's secret cleared, the holder's DID set, `adoptedAt` stamped. Every standing mandate is revoked and the holder's opening mandate is issued the way admission's is. Delegation credentials are left unverified until the holder countersigns them. Refusals, in order: no such resident; already held; the DID already holds a principal in this city; tick; signature; the opening mandate. Arrears and open disputes are not refusals — the holder takes the seat as it stands. The chain row is `adoption:resident:adopted`, written first. Adoption is not reversible: an adopted resident leaves through `leave` like any holder and returns through `admit` — there is no release back to the world's mind. A holder leaving a household of several leaves the household standing — no vacate, no escrow, the leaver takes 0 from the shared purse.

**[friday22 note]** Two of the `[assumed]` lines carry weight the principal has not weighed: "takes 0" on leaving a family — a holder who spent from a shared purse walks out with nothing of it, which is one reading of "the purse stays shared" and not the only one; and "not reversible" answers the adoption entry's open question "whether an adopted resident can be released back to the world's mind" as no, by mechanics rather than by his word. Both are cheap to change until the first adoption lands on a chain.

**Answered 2026-09-13** — both questions put to the principal, in his words as the options were put: (1) a holder leaving a family — **"Takes nothing; the purse stays with the family"** (over: takes its own wages earned while held; an equal share — neither taken); (2) reversibility — **"Not reversible — leaving is the only way out"** (over: a signed release back to the world's mind — not taken). Both `[confirmed]`.

**Promoted 2026-09-13** — in the `[assumed]` mechanics paragraph above, "Adoption is not reversible: an adopted resident leaves through `leave` like any holder and returns through `admit` — there is no release back to the world's mind" and "the leaver takes 0 from the shared purse" are now `[confirmed]` on the answer above; the rest of that paragraph stays `[assumed]`. The adoption entry's open question "whether an adopted resident can be released back to the world's mind" is closed: no.

### 2026-09-13 — [assumed] Adoption, built — and what jarvis11 corrected (commit `117ad2d`)

**Chose:** lane D landed. The principal's six answers — by adoption; the engine decides nothing for the seat from then on; history, job and home kept; every seat claimable including a member of a family and a child's; not reversible; a leaver from a family takes nothing — are the two `[confirmed]` adoption entries above. Everything below is the coordinator's shape beyond those six words, from `city/DESIGN_NOTES_V2.md` §7 "Phase 7 — adoption" and the commit; the principal chose the law, not these mechanics.

**(a) The door — `adopt(world, resident, did, tick, signature, opening)` in `city/src/sim/systems/economy/adoption.ts`.** One invocable law, unscheduled — a world nobody adopts into is byte for byte the world before; pins unmoved. The body is `adoptBody(realm, resident, did, tick, seq)`, `seq` the resident's personal agent's `acts`, single-use as the kill switch and leaving count it; presented with the same opening intent admission offers (`openingDraft`/`openingProblemFor` now shared by both doors). Six refusals in order, nothing written: no such resident; a seat already held; a DID already on the table — one seat per identity per city, a departed one included; another tick; the signature; the opening. Arrears and open disputes are not refusals; age is not asked. Writes: `adoption:resident:adopted` first; every valid mandate of every agent of the principal revoked; the principal turned in place — same `Principal.id`, `did`/`publicKey` overwritten, `secret` emptied, **`Agent.controller` turned to the DID on every agent**, `adoptedAt` stamped; then the holder's opening, `signedBy: 'holder'`. `Firm.founder` unchanged, so `ownerOf` still reads the household. Delegation credentials stay as the engine signed them and `delegationVerifies` answers false until the holder countersigns. No release door.

**(b) The till is fail-closed for any principal whose key is outside — jarvis11's finding.** `settle()` (`market.ts`) had no holder check: when the gate answered escalate or found no mandate it transferred anyway and wrote `counter:purchase:principal-paid` with `humanPresent: 1` — for an admitted holder, spending its own account after its intent lapsed; for an adopted member, spending the family purse in the holder's name with the chain saying a human stood at the till. Closed: for a principal whose key lives outside, anything short of `allow` is a refusal, nothing moves, no row — bills included; a holder widens its own intent. The cupboard is filled by the members the world still answers for; a household of one goes without, which is the holder's own silence.

**(c) `payerOf` — who pays a household's Friday.** A household's bills and its rent's tenant line are paid by `payerOf(world, hh)` (`owner.ts`): the first present member the world still answers for, not the seat an outside key took — so which seat was adopted does not decide whether a family pays. The holder is the payer only when every present member is a holder's, and then the refusal in (b) is its own.

**(d) Leaving a shared household, and the return.** `householdIsShared` (`departure.ts`): a holder leaving a household of several leaves alone — no debts asked, nothing vacated, nothing abroad; the row says `shared`, `owed` and `took` nought, `Household.departed` never set; what goes is the holder's own — the job with the week's minutes forfeited, every agent under `holder-departed`, the resident's mark. **A return to a standing household lifts the marks only — no empty dwelling asked of the city, nothing brought back.**

**(e) The eight departed-member gates.** Building (d) showed "gone means gone" had been kept by the household mark more than the resident's: the dole, the morning's hiring, the till's payer, the dividend line, the pantry's count, the nightly step count, and the 23:59 summary (which had reached every departed resident all along, hidden by a full ring) each now `departed`-gated, with `headOf`/`presentMembers` where a law speaks to one member or counts mouths — provably identical on a world nobody left.

**(f) The seat the benches and vectors adopt.** Yusuf Rossi, resident 0 on seed 42 at 2,000 — the first adult with a job and no firm (not already a holder's, in `validate --holders`). The catalogue carries `adopt` as the thirteenth door (two bodies; 31 vectors, 13 refusals; `vectors.json` byte-identical run to run — the till fix moved later world hashes, subjects unchanged); `docs/DOORS.md` has its row; the holders bench brings its last holder in by adoption ("admitted 2 of 2, adoptions 1 of 1"; `--holders 0` byte-identical); the port bench's step 8 has Ida's own key refused (one seat per identity) and a second key adopt Yusuf, then reads both inboxes. Tests: `city/tests/adoption.test.ts`.

**Over:** a fail-open till for holders (jarvis11 — the chain said a human paid when none did); the adopted seat as the household's payer by default (jarvis11 — the adopted seat would decide whether a family eats); a return to a standing family through the cheapest empty dwelling (a dwelling nobody needs); a release door (rejected by the principal); refusing arrears or disputes at adoption (the holder takes the seat as it stands — the coordinator's, under "keeps its history").

**Because:** the principal's six answers; D-2 — from adoption on, nothing in that seat is the engine's to decide, so the till must refuse rather than guess; D-3 — a `principal-paid` row with `humanPresent: 1` that no human signed is a chain that lies.

**Reversal cost:** cheap for (e), (f) — gates and a bench choice. Moderate for (b), (c) — the fail-closed till and `payerOf` are now what the shared purse means in practice, and a holder's family has eaten under them. Expensive for (a) once a seat is adopted on a live chain: same as the `[confirmed]` entry — taking the seat back is the city seizing a domicile it granted.

**Depends on:** "[confirmed] Residents hold keys by adoption" and "[confirmed] Adoption reaches every seat" (both 2026-09-13) — the six answers; "[assumed] Leaving and the return, built" (2026-09-12) — `leave`, escrow, "gone means gone", now with a shared-household branch; "[assumed] The kill switch, built" (2026-09-12) — `acts`, the single-use body; "[assumed] P5 — the holder countersigns" (2026-09-12) — why the delegation stays unverified; "[assumed] Lane B — the door catalogue" and "[assumed] Lane A — `validate --holders N`" (both 2026-09-13); D-1; D-2; D-3.

**[friday22 note]** Three of these carry weight the principal has not weighed. (b) makes a holder's silence a family's empty cupboard when every present member is a holder's — the shared purse the principal chose to keep shared now has a payer the engine picks by rule. (d)'s return "lifts the marks only" takes the passport's age and stage over the seat's own — Open (xxxiv). And (a)'s "a departed one included" means a holder who left a family cannot adopt a second seat in the same city while its first stands empty — one seat per identity is read as ever, not as present. Two open lines from the landing are (xxxiv) and (xxxv) in the open list.

### 2026-09-14 — [confirmed] The governor's seat — named at founding, bounded by the calibration band, paid from the treasury's tax

Four questions put at the pause after adoption, on Open (xxxii) (what a "bank" is) and (xxxiii) (what a governing role is and how it is paid). Three answered in the principal's words; the fourth is open.

**(1) Who holds the governor's seat at first?** **"Named at the city's founding by whoever creates it; nobody named = the constant stands."** Over: the first holder to claim it (a race to the seat); the treasury as an adoptable seat (a seat the engine's mind holds until claimed); elected among holders (no electorate yet). None taken.

**(2) Is the governor bounded?** **"Yes — inside the calibration band; a change outside is refused."** For the welcome credit the band is half a month to two months of the median wage. The band is a law; the number inside it is policy. Over: no bounds (a governor could set a credit of zero or a hundred months); zero to two months (zero would let a governor close the door by policy). Neither taken.

**(3) How is the governor paid?** **"A share of the treasury's monthly tax, like the residents' dividend."** The share *number* is not chosen; the coordinator will propose one, `[assumed]`, when pay is built — Open (xxxvii). Over: not yet (a seat with no pay — leaves the principal's "profit from governing the city" unbuilt); a fee per admission (pays the governor to admit, which sets the seat against the door). Neither taken.

**(4) Which numbers become policy first?** The principal asked for a simpler explanation: **"I did not understand this part explain to me in simpler way let's talk this problem together."** The coordinator explained the six candidate numbers — the welcome credit (`welcome_credit_months`), the dispute close (30 days, `READING_STANDS_DAYS`), the rating life (90 days, `RATING_LIFE`), the rent index (2 %/month), the owner dividend (15 %), the fiscal anchor — and recommended the welcome credit alone first, the others staying the engine's constants until each is asked for. **No answer yet — open, the principal's; Open (xxxvi).** Nothing beyond the welcome credit is to be made policy on the coordinator's word.

**Chose:** a governor per city, a DID named at founding or nobody; a policy is a signed act inside a band the calibration entry fixes; the seat earns a share of the tax.

**Because:** the principal's, from "[confirmed] The re-asked four" (2026-09-13): economic policies "could change because it is gona managed by different banks"; "agents … governing itself making a profit from governing the city". D-2: the law says a city may be governed and what the governor may not exceed; an agent takes the seat and earns from it. "Nobody named = the constant stands" keeps every existing world byte-identical.

**Reversal cost:** cheap until a city is founded with a governor named — the unset case is the world today. Moderate after: a policy row on a chain was a signed act under a band; widening or narrowing the band later re-judges acts already taken. Expensive for pay once a governor has drawn from the tax — taking it back is the city reclaiming a dividend it paid.

**Depends on:** "[confirmed] The re-asked four: two refinements" (2026-09-13) — both directions; "[confirmed] A newcomer's first money is a welcome credit from the city, a month of the median wage" (2026-09-11) — the first number that becomes policy; "[confirmed] The fiscal anchor" (2026-09-09) — a candidate for policy, not decided; D-1; D-2; D-3.

**`[assumed]` — tuesday22's lane plan, adopted by the coordinator, not put to the principal beyond the three answers above:**
- A `Policy` record on the world, **absent when unset** — a world with no governor and no policy is byte-identical to today's.
- `governor: DID | null`, set from `WorldOptions.governor` at founding; `null` means the constant stands and every `setPolicy` is refused.
- The door: `setPolicy(world, by, field, value, tick, signature)` over the body `policyBody(realm, field, value, tick, seq)`.
- Refusals, in order, nothing written: no principal for `by`; the key lives in the world (not a holder's); not the governor; no such field; the value not finite or outside `[low, high]` from the calibration entry; another tick; the signature.
- The chain row `policy:changed {field, old, new, by, seq, signedBy: 'holder'}`, written first.
- `welcomeCreditMonths` read at the tick in `city/src/sim/systems/economy/admission.ts`, never cached — an offer at the door reads the policy standing at that tick.
- The catalogue kind `setPolicy` in the door catalogue, one vector, the benches updated.
- Pay is a later step, not in this lane.

**[friday22 note]** Three things in the `[assumed]` plan go beyond the three answers. "Nobody named = the constant stands" was said of the seat, not of the door — refusing every `setPolicy` when `governor` is null is one reading; another is that a founder may name a governor later by a signed act, which the plan has no door for. The band `[low, high]` is read from the calibration entry, which makes the band a calibration file's, editable by whoever edits `assumptions/` — the principal said "the band is a law". And a governor whose key is a holder's is subject to the kill switch and to `leave`; what a city's policy is when its governor has left — stands, or reverts to the constant — is not asked. ### 2026-09-14 — [assumed] Attraction, built — Gemini on the page, the governor's seat, the prospectus (commit 8177c8f)

Three pieces landed in one commit — "Living World: attraction - Gemini's reactions on the page, the governor's seat (the welcome credit as a signed policy), and the prospectus a city publishes" — recorded from `city/DESIGN_NOTES_V2.md` §7's last three Phase 7 entries. The principal's words in this lane are the three answers under "[confirmed] The governor's seat" (2026-09-14) and nothing more; every choice below is the coordinator's, `[assumed]`, and put to him here.

**Chose:** the seat built for one field (the welcome credit), as the principal said and no other on the coordinator's word; Gemini's recorded answers on the observatory page, chosen by name from a manifest; a prospectus per city that derives every number from a law's existing reading and computes none of its own.

**`[assumed]` — five choices beyond the three answers:**

- **(a) Who exercises the seat.** The seat is named by DID at founding, but the DID alone cannot sign: it is exercised by a principal *here* — admitted or adopted — and is **frozen while that principal's agent is not active** (halted by the kill switch, or departed), the standing number staying until a resume or a return on the same passport reopens it. jarvis11's read found the hole (a governor who had left still governed, the principal row persisting with an empty secret); this is the fix, and it answers the `[friday22 note]` question above ("what a city's policy is when its governor has left — stands, or reverts") with *stands, frozen*. Over: reverting to the constant on departure (would let a leaving governor reset a city's offer by walking out). `economy/policy.ts`.
- **(b) The band.** Closed, `[low, high]` inclusive — "half a month to two months" — and it is the **calibration entry's own** `low`/`high`, read through `calibration.ts`'s `band` door; an entry with no band is not a policy. Moving the band is an edit to `assumptions/`, which **re-judges rows already on the chain**; a restore refuses a snapshot whose policy sits outside the band it now reads, as it refuses a founding record that disagrees. The principal said "the band is a law"; the coordinator placed the law in the calibration file, not in code — the `[friday22 note]` above stands on this.
- **(c) The recording's `generation` settings.** Thinking budget 0 for a one-line judgement (the first pass came back "APPROVE because it" — thinking counted against the output budget); a cut answer (`finishReason`) is refused by the recorder; the file header carries `generation` so a thinking-on and a thinking-off recording are distinguishable. The field is **advisory**. **The shipped Gemini recording is NOT re-recorded** even though it lacks the field: re-asking the same 21 questions at temperature 0 reworded four answers, which would move the pinned hash (`53a6a7e77a02a0ab` at 2,880 ticks) — the whole reason an answer is recorded once and committed. The next deliberate re-record carries it.
- **(d) The prospectus names no other product.** No AP2, no ERC-8004 inside the JSON — facts with sources and no comparison — **against the verified research's own recommendation** (`docs/research/2026-09-14-attraction-*.md`, yolo's leads and solo's verdicts) to name them in the offer. The research file holds the comparison; the offer holds the city's own reading. Over: the research's recommendation (a comparison in a signed offer is a claim about someone else's product that no row of ours evidences). **Confirmed 2026-09-14** — "[confirmed] Four answers at the pause after attraction", (4): "Keep the offer to our own facts; comparisons live in the research file." No longer `[assumed]`.
- **(e) A verdict is attached only to the world it was made on.** The validate report needs the same country and seed; the vectors need the exact realm. A prospectus that let Dorsk wear a lone Halvik run's PASS/FAIL would be the offer saying something about a city nothing ran on. The report on disk is FAILING on the rent band; the founded-to-its-customers town reads 70 % unemployment and 58 % rent-to-income; the prospectus reports both and judges neither.

**Observed, not decided:** all 21 Gemini answers approve, several quoting the system prompt's "the engine has already applied every rule and cap" back as their reason. Changing that sentence moves the fingerprint — a re-record. Whether the prompt should stop telling the model the engine has already applied the rules is **Open (xxxviii)**, not taken on the coordinator's word. Also observed: the full suite at 8177c8f was 1008 of 1010 — the worker seam not rebuilding the vectors' world with its governor, and `Object.keys` in a hashed tree — fixed in `94587ec`, the commit that follows.

**Because:** the principal's three answers (2026-09-14) and "[confirmed] The re-asked four" (2026-09-13); D-1 — the prospectus is domicile written where `curl` can read it; D-2 — the law says what the governor may not exceed, the seat is an agent's to hold. (a)–(e) are the coordinator's readings of those, none put to him.

**Reversal cost:** (a) cheap — no world yet has a governor whose agent has halted or left; the freeze is a refusal, not a row. (b) moderate — a band moved in `assumptions/` re-judges every `policy:changed` row already signed under the old band, and a restore that refuses is a world that will not load. (c) cheap — a re-record is one bench run; expensive only in that it moves the federation hash every test pins. (d) cheap — a field added to JSON. (e) cheap — the attachment is a check at derivation time.

**Depends on:** "[confirmed] The governor's seat — named at founding, bounded by the calibration band, paid from the treasury's tax" (2026-09-14) and its `[assumed]` lane plan; "[confirmed] Adoption reaches every seat" (2026-09-13) — the adopted principal who may hold the seat; "[confirmed] The kill switch" and "[confirmed] Leaving: a signed act" (both 2026-09-12) — what freezes it; "[assumed] Ownership, landed" (2026-09-11) for `welcomeAffordable`; "Minds: the first Gemini recording" (commit `4b3ab66`) — the recording (c) does not re-record; Open (xxxvi) — the welcome credit is the only field; Open (xxxvii) — pay not built.

### 2026-09-14 — [confirmed] Rents priced against net at founding — the rent-to-income root cause fixed and the pins re-pinned; laws and policies must stay very light, the city ready for 10,000 agents, millions over time

**The question put.** The rent-to-income check fails — 49.5 % against the 20–40 % band — because `founding.ts` step 5 prices the rent ladder so the median rent is `rent_to_income_target` (0.30) of the median earning household's GROSS month, while the target's own unit (`assumptions/housing_nordholm/rent_to_income_target`) says NET, and the check reads `incomeTotal`, which `labour.ts` accrues net of income tax. Income tax runs at `TAX_RATE_CAP` 0.40 because the town founded to its customers has 363 of the 1,000 calibrated jobs, so the civic payroll and the dole for the idle 637 fall on a third of the wage base. 0.30 gross ÷ 0.60 = 0.50 net — the 49.5 %. The measurement is the comment beside the pin in `city/tests/validate.test.ts` (lane F, "measure, do not touch", 2026-09-14): 55.3 / 49.5 / 44.3 % at 7 / 30 / 120 days, seed 42, 2,000 residents; the rent index never touches an occupied rent (`lowerEmpty` skips them; the raise needs zero empties, and seed 42 has 70), so the index as written cannot carry the number into the band. Options: (1) fix it — price against net at founding, re-pin the three determinism hashes with provenance — recommended; (2) fix both the net pricing and the under-founding; (3) leave it failing, pinned as known.

A second move to revert would be the same cost again and would put the unit error back. Recorded once.

### 2026-09-14 — [confirmed] A return lifts the marks only — the seat keeps its own age and history; the family purse is the household's, finer splits are the agents' own agreements

**The questions put.** Open (xxxiv) and (xxxv), both raised by the adoption landing ("[assumed] Adoption, built — and what jarvis11 corrected", 2026-09-13, the note under it) and measured before being put, lane E, 2026-09-14 — the readings asserted as readings, not rules, in `city/tests/adoption.test.ts` ("Open (xxxiv) — measured before deciding") and `city/tests/leaving.test.ts` ("Open (xxxv) — measured before deciding").

**(xxxiv) measured** — a child's adopted seat that leaves cannot return as a child: the door tests the passport's age at rule 4 (working age), not the seat's. A working-age passport on the same DID is a return, and `returnHolder` overwrites the seat's age and stage from the passport — the child comes back an adult aged 36.

**(xxxv) measured** — in a household of two adopted seats, the first leaving is shared (takes 0, the household stands); the second leaving takes the whole purse abroad (`hh.abroad` is the household's, not the leaver's); whichever returns first is offered the entire escrow, re-forms the home, and brings it all back to the shared account; the other returns to a household that stayed.

**The principal's answers, verbatim as the options were put:** (xxxiv) **"A return lifts the marks only; the seat keeps its own age and history"** (over: keep today's behaviour — the passport overwrites the seat's age and stage; not taken). (xxxv) **"whichever agentic aggrements that will be sufficnt to be understandbale"**.

**Chose:** (xxxiv) **DECIDED.** A return lifts the leaver's marks and nothing else; the seat's own age, stage and history stand. The passport's age is the door's business at rule 4 and stops there — it is not written onto the seat. The child's seat that left as a child and returns is therefore still the child's seat at its own age; whether such a return is admitted at all (rule 4 refuses a child's-age passport, and a working-age passport now no longer rewrites the seat) is a consequence for the builder to state, not a new rule. (xxxv) **DECIDED by default, the reading flagged.** `[assumed]` — the coordinator's reading of the answer: the engine keeps the simplest understandable default — the purse is the household's; whoever returns first re-forms the home and brings the whole escrow back (today's behaviour, now explicit rather than accidental). Any finer split — per leaver, per wages earned, per share — is an agreement among the agents themselves, made and honoured outside the engine, which D-2 leaves to them. The principal has **not** chosen the per-leaver alternative; he answered that whatever agentic agreements are sufficient to be understandable will do.

**Over:** (xxxiv) keep today's behaviour — the passport overwrites the seat — not taken; it lets a return rewrite a resident's history, which is the chain saying something other than what happened. (xxxv) the engine splitting escrow per leaver (each takes what it took, each brings back its own) — not chosen; not refused either — it is simply not what he said, and D-2 says the engine does not decide who gets what of a family's money when the agents can agree it themselves.

**Because:** (xxxiv) the principal's words: the seat keeps its own age and history. (xxxv) the principal's words, read by the coordinator: sufficient and understandable — the household purse is the simplest understandable default and it is the one already on the chain; agreements above it are the agents'.

**Reversal cost:** (xxxiv) cheap now — the change is in `returnHolder` and no live chain has a returned child's seat; moderate once a return has been written under the new rule, because reverting would rewrite a seat's age on a chain that recorded it as kept. (xxxv) cheap — nothing changes in the engine; making the per-leaver split an engine rule later means an escrow ledger keyed by leaver, moderate, and it takes back a choice D-2 gave the agents.

**Depends on:** "[confirmed] Adoption reaches every seat: the shared purse stays shared; a child's seat is claimable" (2026-09-13) — a leaving holder takes nothing, the purse stays with the family; "[assumed] Adoption, built — and what jarvis11 corrected" (2026-09-13) — (d) "lifts the marks only", now read as the principal meant it; "[assumed] Leaving and the return, built" (2026-09-12) — escrow, `hh.abroad`, "gone means gone"; "[confirmed] Residents hold keys by adoption" (2026-09-13); D-1 (2026-09-06); D-2 (2026-09-10) — the economy, including a family's agreements about its money, is the agents'; D-4 (2026-09-14) — a per-leaver escrow ledger is the kind of weight a law should not carry unasked.

**[friday22 note]** (xxxv) is decided by default, not by his word: "whichever agentic agreements that will be sufficient to be understandable" is not a choice between the household purse and a per-leaver split — it says the agents may agree what they like so long as it is understandable. The coordinator's reading keeps the engine as it is and names it. If a holder ever asks the engine to enforce a family agreement about escrow, that is the moment (xxxv) comes back to the principal as a real question. On (xxxiv), the builder should report what now happens to a child's seat presenting a working-age passport — admitted at its own age, or refused because the seat is a child's — since the answer settles the seat's record but not the door's verdict.

**Built 2026-09-15, the door's verdict (jarvis11 asked it be stated):** on a return, rule 4 (working age) is NOT applied — the passport identifies only (shape, signature, city); a child's seat that left returns as the child at its own age on a passport of any well-formed age, and is not marked seeking until of working age; `[assumed]` the seat also keeps its values and posture (posture moves only under the incident/recovery law until a signed posture door exists). The entry's parenthetical "rule 4 refuses a child's-age passport" described the old behaviour; the header of `city/src/sim/systems/economy/admission.ts` item 5 now states the new.

**(xxxv) 2026-09-15:** `city/tests/leaving.test.ts` now asserts the household-purse behaviour as the rule (relabelled from "a reading"): the escrow is the household's; the first to return brings it all back.

### 2026-09-14 — [confirmed] Housing for the port: a vacancy stock the governor sets, and a law under which agents build dwellings for money

**The question put.** How new dwellings come to exist. mage's read-only measurement, 2026-09-14: seed 42 founds 724 dwellings, 70 of them empty; admission needs a wholly empty building (`admission.ts`, `cheapestEmptyDwelling`); the 71st holder through the port is refused "no dwelling stands empty"; 10,000 holders need about 14× the stock. The sim itself is cheap — a 10,000-resident year is about 30 CPU-minutes, per-tick cost is O(residents) in market/schedule/movement, the `agents` slice is 2 % — so housing is the first wall the port hits, not the engine. Options put: only a building law (agents build, the city founds nothing more); only the generator (the founding lays more empties, no law); shared seats (holders double up in a dwelling); both a governor's vacancy policy and a building law.

**The principal's answer, verbatim as the options were put:** **"Both: the city founds a vacancy stock as a governor's policy, AND a law lets agents build dwellings for money"**.

**Chose:** both. (a) A **vacancy stock** the city keeps as a governor's policy — a target of empty dwellings the founding lays and the city maintains, signed by the governor's seat like the welcome credit, bounded by the calibration band. This is the **second `Policy` field**; the principal's "welcome credit only for now; add the others one at a time as I approve each" (Open (xxxvi), decided 2026-09-14) covers this one by this answer — no third field is implied. (b) A **building law** under which an agent builds a dwelling for money — the economy's own supply of housing, D-2's answer to "could an agent do this under a law?". The law's shape is Open (xli), the principal's before it is built.

**Over:** **only a building law** — not taken; a port that refuses the 71st holder until some agent decides to build is a port closed at launch, and the principal wants a fast start. **Only the generator** — not taken; a city that founds every dwelling forever is scaffolding, the thing D-2 says is never the product. **Shared seats** — not taken; it changes what a dwelling and a household are to make room, instead of making room.

**Because:** D-2 — the agents build the economy, and housing is economy; the policy is the fast start that keeps the port open until they do. D-4 — laws stay very light and the city is to be ready for 10,000; a vacancy target is one number the governor signs, and a building law is one act per building, neither a walk over agents.

**Reversal cost:** (a) cheap before a chain carries the field — one `Policy` field and one founding constant; moderate after — a signed policy row on a live chain cannot be unsigned, only superseded by a new one. (b) cheap until built (nothing exists); moderate once dwellings stand that agents paid for — removing the law leaves built rows with no law that made them.

**Depends on:** "[confirmed] The governor's seat — named at founding, bounded by the calibration band, paid from the treasury's tax" (2026-09-14) — the seat that signs the vacancy target; "[confirmed] Four answers at the pause after attraction" (2026-09-14), Open (xxxvi) — one policy field at a time as the principal approves each, this answer approving the second; D-2 (2026-09-10) — agents build the economy; D-4 (2026-09-14) — light laws, ready for 10,000; "[confirmed] Rents reprice to vacancy, slowly" (2026-09-09) — the index a maintained vacancy stock will keep pressing downward, to be measured, not assumed.

**[friday22 note]** Two things the answer does not settle. The vacancy target's number and its band are not chosen — a policy field with no number is a field; the coordinator proposes one `[assumed]` beside the calibration and puts it to the principal, as with the governor's 5 %. And "a law lets agents build dwellings for money" names who acts and that money moves; it does not name who may build, on what, at what cost, to whom the money goes, or what the new dwelling's rent is — that is (xli), and none of it is to be built on the coordinator's word.

### 2026-09-15 — [assumed] The city–registry signing seam, measured before Q-3 (jarvis22's read)

**What was measured, not decided.** jarvis22's read-only check, 2026-09-14/15, of how the city's signed objects stand against the spec's uniform signing rule, taken before Q-3 (which chain carries an outside agent's admission) is put to the principal. Findings, as facts:

- **§4 rules 2–3 already hold byte for byte.** The city's `ed25519:` paths compute `passportDigest` as `sha256:` + hex and sign over the UTF-8 digest string (`city/src/agents/identity.ts:248-355`). The signature encoding — base64url, 86 characters, `ed25519:` prefix — matches §3. The `did:key` form — multicodec `ed01`, multibase `z` — is identical (`identity.ts:124-213`). Canonicalisation (`city/src/sim/snapshot/codec.ts:111-174`) agrees with JCS on every fixture the tests exercise; **no unicode or surrogate-pair fixture exists yet**, so that agreement is measured only as far as the fixtures go.
- **The one real difference is §4 rule 1 — what the body is.** The spec's body is the object minus `signature`. Every city door signs a **projection** built by its own body function — `mandateBody`, `passportBody`, `disputeBody`, `haltBody`, `ratingBody`, `escalationAnswerBody`, `adoptBody`, `leaveBody`, `policyBody` — and several add an `action` discriminator that is not on the stored object. A generic spec verifier therefore cannot rebuild a city body from the stored object without a per-kind table.
- **What a bridge (C1, waiting on Q-3) must do.** City→registry: rebuild the body with the city's body function, then verify with the spec's primitives — the signatures are already spec-shaped, nothing is re-signed. Registry→city: `verify(canonicalPayload(objectMinusSignature), registry, signature)` already works (`city/tests/agents.identity.test.ts:128-153`).
- **Gaps.** The spec's `admission_record` fixture is exercised by no city test (`city/src/agents/registry.ts` does not exist). The `registration` row mapping (registry, operator, modelHash, policyHash, at ← tick) still fits the fixture one to one — but city-minted rows carry `rule:` / `household-consumption:` labels and a `<country>-agent-registry` string where a unified validator expects `sha256:` and `did:key`: two vocabularies in one column. `record_hash?` is not yet on the row. The two vector files share DIDs and signature strings but not shape: the city's are replay vectors with prose `reasonPrefix` and FNV-64 world hashes; the spec's are pure crypto vectors with snake_case tokens; the key-seed namespaces differ (`living-city:passport:` vs `agentsociety-fixture-v0:`).

**Chose `[assumed]`:** the **projection design is kept**. The mandate's `used` and `revokedAt` mutate after issue, so object-minus-signature cannot be the mandate's body without the signature breaking on first use; the projection is what lets a signed row live on a chain that later writes to it. The **per-kind body table is the bridge's cost, not the doors'** — C1 carries a table of nine body functions; no door changes what it signs.

**Over:** re-signing every city door over object-minus-signature to match §4 rule 1 literally — not taken; it breaks the mandate on its own `used` counter and moves every pinned hash for a difference the bridge can carry in one table. Changing the spec's rule 1 to admit projections — not taken on the coordinator's word; the spec wins until the principal says the runtime is right, and here the runtime is not shown wrong, only different at the seam.

**Because:** the coordinator's reading of D-3 (one gate: an outside verifier with `curl` and the validator) and of "[assumed] Spec v0.1.0: … credentials round-trip through ASE" (2026-09-11) — the seam is where the outside verifier meets the city, and the measurement says the primitives already agree, so the cheapest honest bridge is a body table, not a re-signing. Nothing here is the principal's word.

**Reversal cost:** cheap now — no bridge exists, no city row has crossed to a registry; the table is not written. Moderate once C1 is built and rows have been verified across it — reverting to object-minus-signature would re-sign live chains and re-pin every hash. Adding a unicode/surrogate canonicalisation fixture is cheap and should precede C1.

**Depends on:** "[assumed] Governance lane A7: the Identity Registry" (2026-09-12) — (b) the admission record's shape and (e) the outside-DID scope that is Q-3; "[assumed] The holder signs — lanes P1–P3" and "[assumed] P5" (2026-09-12) — the body functions measured; "[assumed] Spec v0.1.1: a rotated key is the DID's only if the chain attests it" (2026-09-12); "[assumed] Lane B — the door catalogue" (2026-09-13) — the nine doors the table would enumerate; D-3 (2026-09-11); Q-3 (`docs/OPEN_QUESTIONS.md`), open.

**Open.** **Q-3** — which chain carries an admitted outside agent's admission — is the gate for C1; this entry measures the seam so that Q-3 can be put with the bridge's cost known, and decides nothing Q-3 decides. **(xlii)** — whether city-minted `registration` rows should carry `sha256:` policy hashes and a `did:key` registry, or keep their `rule:` / `household-consumption:` labels and the `<country>-agent-registry` string — is the principal's, to be put with the other session alongside Q-3; until then the row keeps its labels and no validator is taught to accept both.

**[friday22 note]** The "kept" projection is a reading of what the code already does, recorded so it is not later mistaken for a spec-conformant body. The spec-vs-runtime disagreement at §4 rule 1 is real and is not papered over here: the spec still says object-minus-signature, the city still signs projections, and the bridge is proposed as the place that absorbs it. If the principal would rather the spec admit projections (a rule-1 change, trevor's lane) or the city sign literally (a re-sign, every hash moving), that is a decision, not a build, and it goes to him with Q-3.

- **2026-09-15 — the unicode gap above is closed.** A city test, `city/tests/agents.identity.jcs.test.ts`, now replays the spec's content-reference vectors, the Unicode / surrogate-pair one included, through the city's canonicaliser (the other session's request). Canonicalisation agreement is no longer measured "only as far as the fixtures go"; the §4 rule 1 projection difference stands as recorded.

### 2026-09-15 — [confirmed] The building law's shape; a newcomer takes any free household slot; and three directions — mixed-use buildings, a self-funding governance, a world that grows with investment

**The questions put.** (1) Open (xli), the building law's shape, as one proposal: a holder, or a firm's holder, builds **one dwelling per signed act** on a **vacant plot the seed placed at founding** — no free placement; the builder picks the kind; the cost is a calibration number, "ten years of that kind's rent", with a band, plus the entry law's 1 % fee to the treasury; the build is **instant** — no timer, no per-tick work; the builder's account is the landlord, rent opens at the going rate and follows the vacancy law; no clawback, no price band; when the plots run out the door refuses. (2) Room to share: admission today needs a wholly empty building (`admission.ts`, `cheapestEmptyDwelling`), while the founding houses 4 households per low-rise and 8 per mid-rise — whether a newcomer may take any free household slot, as residents do.

**The principal's answers.** (1) **"Yes, this shape."** (2) Yes — a newcomer takes any free household slot like residents do. (3) His addition, verbatim: **"yes this works but also agents can choose to use those house as offices or business centers and aslo they lower level stores shoudl be a market liek toronto city design as well as singapore city as well , ( use roman and jewsih agentic laws to make the governance to make money itlsef so it can govern and also just in case mayeb in crease he size of the planet size of the world becasue people can inevst on the world to becoem more and more"**.

**Chose:** (1) the building law as proposed — one dwelling per signed act, on a founding-placed vacant plot, kind the builder's, cost "ten years of that kind's rent" (a calibration number with a band) plus the 1 % entry fee, instant, builder's account the landlord, rent at the going rate under the vacancy law, no clawback, no price band, the door refuses when plots are gone. (2) Admission fills **any free household slot** in a standing building; a wholly empty building is no longer required. (3) Three **standing directions**, recorded in the Architect Mode table as D-5, D-6, D-7: **D-5 mixed-use** — a building's use is its holder's choice (home, office, business) under one light law, with street level as market frontage on the Toronto / Singapore pattern; **D-6 self-funding governance** — the treasury's income laws to be modelled on the Roman (municipal charters, vectigalia, publicani) and Jewish (kehilla self-taxation, takkanot, heter iska) traditions, a research lane (yolo scouts, solo verifies) before any design; **D-7 a growing world** — land is not a fixed cap; the map may grow when investment pays for it.

**Over:** (1) **a build time** — not taken; a timer is per-tick work per building, the weight D-4 forbids, and nothing in the offer needs the wait. **A land levy** — not taken; a recurring charge on plots is a walk over holders every period, and the one-off cost plus the 1 % fee already pays the treasury at the act. (2) **A whole building or nothing** — not taken; it refuses a newcomer while seats stand empty and makes the vacancy stock count buildings when the founding counts households.

**Because:** (1) and (2) D-2 — housing is economy and the agents supply it under a law; D-4 — one act, one row, no timer, no levy, no per-tick work; the plots the seed placed keep the layout deterministic and the door honest about scarcity. (3) In his words: agents choose how a house is used; the street level is a market; governance should "make money itself so it can govern"; the world may grow "because people can invest on the world".

**Reversal cost:** (1) cheap until built; moderate once dwellings stand that agents paid for and let — the rows and their rents are on the chain. (2) cheap — one predicate in admission; moderate once chains carry newcomers seated in partly-filled buildings, since the old rule would have refused them. (3) D-5 to D-7 are directions, not laws — cheap to reverse now, nothing built; each becomes a decision only when its law is put to the principal.

A design pass; the principal's after a proposal. **(xliv) The mixed-use law's shape.** What changes when a dwelling becomes a shop or an office — jobs, rent, the market index, and what the street-level frontage rule is. The principal's after a proposal. D-6's research lane precedes any income-law design; its findings return as a proposal, not a build.

**The questions put.** (1) Open (xliii), the growth primitive, as one proposal: **one plot per signed act "invest in the world"**, placed by the founding's own placement rule on unbuilt road-touching land in a named district; **infill first** — new districts and new roads are a second primitive, later; the signer is the **governor now, holders once buildings are owned**; the cost is a calibration number, **three years of the city's median rent per plot, band 1–10**, `[assumed]`; the entry law's **1 % fee to the treasury**; the rest **leaves the city as materials to the abroad purse**, counted on the boundary row; the world hash **moves only by the act**; restore **replays the acts through the seed's allocator**; **no timer**; the door **refuses when the named district has no free land**. (2) Open (xliv), the mixed-use law's shape, as one proposal: **one signed `declareUse`**; a home gains a **ground-floor shop or office frontage as a second building id** sharing the base's rect and door; the **households and their rent are untouched**; frontage jobs **≤ 2× the floor area and ≤ the kind's count**; the entry law founds a firm there unchanged; an **occupied home cannot be converted whole, an empty one can**; declaring costs the **1 % fee**; **civic kinds excluded**; the hash moves by the act only.

**The principal's answers.** (1) **"Yes — infill by signed act now, new districts as the next step."** (2) **"Yes, but a declared shop also owes the holder a premise rent."**

**Chose:** (1) the growth primitive as proposed — infill by signed act, the founding's placement rule, governor now and holders later, three years of median rent per plot (band 1–10, `[assumed]`), 1 % to the treasury, the remainder out as materials on the boundary row, hash moved by the act only, restore by replay through the seed's allocator, no timer, refusal when a district is full; new districts and roads are **the next step**, a second primitive, not folded into this one. (2) the frontage law as proposed — `declareUse`, a second building id on the base's rect and door, households and rent untouched, the job cap, the firm founded under the entry law unchanged, whole conversion only of an empty home, the 1 % fee, civic kinds excluded, hash by the act only — **plus a premise rent**: the firm founded on a declared frontage owes the frontage's holder a rent. The number is not chosen — Open (xlv).

**Over:** (1) **materials bought inside the city at a brickworks** — offered by the coordinator as the better D-2 answer (an agent-built supplier instead of an abroad purse) and **not taken**; the principal went straight to "new districts as the next step" and left the materials abroad. (2) in his words, a declared shop "owes the holder a premise rent" — the frontage is the holder's land let to a firm, and letting is paid, as dwellings are.

**Reversal cost:** (1) cheap until built; moderate once plots stand that an act paid for — the acts are on the chain and restore replays them, so removing the primitive orphans placed buildings. Moving materials from the abroad purse to a city brickworks later is cheap in law (one payee changes) and moderate in the boundary-row accounting already recorded. (2) cheap until built; moderate once frontages are declared and firms founded on them — the second building ids and their rent rows are on the chain. Adding the premise rent before the first declaration is cheap; adding it after is a retroactive charge on standing firms.

**Depends on:** "[confirmed] The building law's shape; a newcomer takes any free household slot; and three directions" (2026-09-15) — D-5 and D-7 are the directions these two laws give shape to; (xliii) and (xliv) were that entry's opens; D-2 (2026-09-10) — the firm on the frontage is the agents', the law is ours; D-4 (2026-09-14) — one act, one row, no timer; the entry law's 1 % fee; "[confirmed] Rents reprice to vacancy, slowly" (2026-09-09); the abroad purse and the boundary row (the port bench across the border, 2026-09-13).

### 2026-09-15 — [confirmed] The city's next income: a transaction duty of 0.02; every other fee is the agents' own agreement

**The question put.** D-6, after solo's verification (`docs/research/2026-09-15-governance-funding-verified.md`: the fee-farm is the opposite contract of the built governor pay; portoria and publicani are real and light; Bava Batra 7b does not rule a flat per-house levy): which income should the city add next — **a customs duty on cross-city trade set by the governor within a band (recommended)**, tax farming by auction, an admission fee (against D-1), or nothing yet.

**The principal's answer, verbatim:** **"something or whatevr it is it shoudl be 0.02 somethign liek this tarncation or whatever eveyr other fees will be set up by agentic colabration"**.

**Chose:** a **transaction duty of 0.02** on cross-city trade as the city's next income, and **every other fee is the agents' own agreement, not engine law**.

**The coordinator's reading `[assumed]`:** a **2 % duty (rate 0.02)** on cross-city trade at the federation's port — the portoria shape — as the next `Policy` field, **`tradeDuty`, default 0.02**, band to propose (e.g. 0–0.05), **collected per shipment to the treasury** as one line where the shipment is already recorded (D-4 light: one act, one row, no walk). **No admission fee and no tax farming built.** Whether "0.02" meant a 2 % rate or an absolute 0.02 per unit is the coordinator's reading (rate) — flagged, not the principal's word. This is the **third `Policy` field the principal has approved one at a time**, after the welcome credit (2026-09-14) and the vacancy target (2026-09-14).

**Over:** **tax farming by auction (publicani)** — not taken now; the principal named one duty and left the rest to the agents; solo's verdict keeps the publicani shape as a later option. **An admission fee** — not taken; against D-1 (domicile must be valuable and leavable, and the door is the offer). **Nothing yet** — not taken; the principal chose a number.

**Because:** in his words — "0.02 something like this, transaction or whatever"; "every other fee will be set up by agentic collaboration". D-2: the city writes the one law that funds governance; the agents agree the rest among themselves. D-6: the city "makes money itself so it can govern".

**Reversal cost:** cheap until built — one field, default 0.02; moderate once shipments carry duty rows to the treasury on the chain, since replay would need the field's history. Changing the rate is a policy change by the governor within the band, not a reversal. Reading "0.02" as an absolute per-unit charge instead of a rate is a cheap correction before the field is signed; expensive after, since every duty row would be wrong.

**Depends on:** D-6 (2026-09-15) and solo's verification (`docs/research/2026-09-15-governance-funding-verified.md`); "[confirmed] Four answers at the pause after attraction" (2026-09-14) — "welcome credit only for now; add the others one at a time as I approve each"; "[confirmed] The governor's seat — named at founding, bounded by the calibration band, paid from the treasury's tax" (2026-09-14); D-1 (2026-09-06); D-2 (2026-09-10); D-4 (2026-09-14); the boundary row and the port bench across the border (2026-09-13) — where the shipment is already recorded.

**Open.** **(xlvi) The duty's band and its exact base** — declared value of the shipment, or the goods' cost? — to propose to the principal before the field is signed.

Both should be put back to the principal in one line before `tradeDuty` is signed. ### 2026-09-15 — [confirmed] The 0.02 is a universal transaction levy, not a customs duty — a very small fee on every transaction funds the world, the cities and the governance; lower in the future

**The question put.** The two flagged readings of the previous entry, put as one line: is the 0.02 (a) **2 % of each cross-city shipment** (the portoria shape, recommended), (b) **2 % of every transaction, in-city included**, or (c) **a flat 0.02 per act**?

**The principal's answer, verbatim:** **"could be lower in the feature imagien thsi sytem in teh future will host 30 billion agents and lets say each oe of tehm will make tarnsaction in between each other i their own currency or huamn levle currency in eenrgy level that very very small fee will make a cocnst development for tehe world and the cities decleopmtn and goevence of teh actula company taht cna invest and make moeny out of it"**.

**Chose:** a **universal levy on every transaction**, taken **in the transaction's own currency** (or a human-level / energy-level currency), at a **very small rate — 0.02 today, lower later**. Its purpose: **constant development of the world, the cities and the governance** — it is **the company's own revenue**, so the company can invest and make money from the system. **Every other fee remains the agents' own agreement** (unchanged from the previous entry).

**Over:** **(a) a customs duty on cross-city shipments only** — rejected; the principal's picture is every agent transacting with every other, not goods crossing a port. **(c) a flat 0.02 per act** — rejected; "very very small fee" on transactions in their own currency reads as a proportion of value, not a unit charge. **Tax farming, an admission fee** — remain not taken (previous entry; D-1).

**Because:** in his words — at 30 billion agents each transacting with each other, "that very very small fee will make a constant development for the world and the cities' development and governance"; it is what "the actual company … can invest and make money out of". D-2: one light law funds the governance; the agents agree the rest. D-6: the governance "makes money itself so it can govern" — now stated as the company's revenue, not only the city treasury's.

**The coordinator's readings `[assumed]`** — to put to the principal with the proposal, none his word:
- **Which transfers count.** Purchases, trade (in-city and cross-city) and premise / dwelling rent — **yes**. Wages, dividends, the tax itself and the treasury's own payments (governor pay, welcome credit) — **no**, so the levy sits on exchange, not on the treasury's own flows.
- **Who pays.** The **payer pays the levy on top** of the price; the payee receives the price in full.
- **The field.** A per-city **`Policy.transactionLevy`**, default 0.02, **band `[0, 0.02]`, movable downward only** — the principal said "lower in the future", never higher.
- **Cost per act (D-4).** **No row per transaction.** An **accumulator** on the transaction's own path and **one row at the month's turn** — otherwise the levy is the heaviest law in the city at exactly the scale it is meant for.
- **The split.** Between the **city's treasury** and a **world / development fund** (the company's) — a number to propose, not yet named.

**Reversal cost:** **expensive** — the levy is the business model; once cities budget on it and the development fund is drawn on, removing it unfunds the governance and the growth (D-7). Lowering the rate is a policy move within the band, not a reversal. Reading corrections (which transfers, payer vs payee) are cheap before the field is signed, expensive after, since every accumulated row would be wrong.

**Depends on:** D-1 (2026-09-06, no admission fee — the levy is not a door charge); D-2 (2026-09-10); D-4 (2026-09-14) — whose scale the principal has now stated as **30 billion agents**, beyond the "3 million" recorded there: the O(1) rule is what makes a universal levy possible at all; D-6 (2026-09-15); "[confirmed] The governor's seat … paid from the treasury's tax" (2026-09-14); "[confirmed] The city's next income: a transaction duty of 0.02" (2026-09-15), superseded in its reading by this entry.

**Open.** **(xlvi), re-stated:** which transactions count, payer or payee, the treasury / development-fund split, and the band — the principal's, after the coordinator's proposal above is put to him in one line.

**[friday22 note]** "Their own currency or human-level currency in energy level" is recorded as said; whether the levy is denominated per transaction currency or converted to one unit for the fund is not decided and is not in the readings above — it should be in the same proposal. The "30 billion agents" line is a scale statement, not a new decision; it is noted under D-4 in the table below rather than given a new D-line. `[assumed]`.

### 2026-09-15 — [assumed] The governor's pay, built — and what jarvis11 corrected (commit pending)

Open (xxxvii) closes: the seat is paid. `city/src/sim/systems/economy/governor.ts`, uncommitted at the time of writing, to be committed today. The principal's words are "5 % of the month's tax" with a band 0–10 %, paid at the month's turn beside the residents' dividend ("[confirmed] Four answers at the pause after attraction", 2026-09-14, (2)); everything below that is the coordinator's, `[assumed]`, and put to him here.

**Chose:** `GOVERNOR_SHARE = 0.05`, band `[0, 0.10]`, as **law constants in code** — not a calibration entry, not a `Policy` field. The governor cannot set its own pay. `Economy.taxThisMonth` accumulates every Friday's withheld income tax **only while a governor is named**; absent otherwise, so every world without a governor hashes as before (pins unmoved). At the month's turn, after the residents' dividend in the same step, `payGovernor` pays `round(base × 0.05)` from the treasury to the seat's household, capped at the treasury's balance with a `short` ref when it cannot cover the full share; the row is `treasury:governor:paid`, by the law. The prospectus `governance.pay` states share, band, base and hour.

**`[assumed]` — the choices beyond the principal's number:**

- **(a) Where the share lives.** In code, as a law, over: a calibration entry (the band in `assumptions/` is where the welcome credit's band sits, but a pay band a governor could argue over belongs to the law); a `Policy` field (would let the seat sign its own raise — against rule 16's "may not change the law it sits under"). Over both.
- **(b) The frozen seat is paid nothing and the base lapses to the treasury.** A seat is frozen when its agent is halted or departed, when the DID is not a principal here, or — jarvis11's correction — when the DID is a principal whose key is in the world (the same holder test the policy door applies). A frozen seat's month pays nothing, and `taxThisMonth` is **not carried forward**: the tax was the city's, not held for a seat nobody sat in. Over: carrying the base forward as an escrow liability across months (a debt the treasury owes to whoever next sits, growing while the seat is empty — a liability no law named).
- **(c) jarvis11's two corrections folded.** First: a closing firm's final-wage tax (`exit.ts`) now accrues to the base like a Friday's — it was withheld to the treasury but missed the accumulator, so the base under-counted every month a firm closed. Second: the seat predicate for pay applies the holder test exactly as the policy door does — one predicate, not two readings of "who sits".

**Observed:** on seed 42 the first turn paid NHK 41,625.95 on a base of NHK 832,519.09. `validate` prints the withheld tax and the pay only where a governor sits.

**Because:** the principal's (2) of 2026-09-14 and "[confirmed] The governor's seat … paid from the treasury's tax" (2026-09-14); D-2 — the seat is an agent's to hold, the law says what it earns; D-4 — one accumulator on a path already walked, one row a month, no per-agent work.

**Reversal cost:** (a) cheap — two constants move file, nothing on the chain names where they came from. (b) moderate — a frozen month's lapsed base is a treasury balance already paid out in later dividends; reverting to escrow would need a row the chain does not have. (c) cheap — corrections to a base, before any recording pins the pay. The pay row itself is on the chain of every world with a governor from this commit on; removing it moves those hashes.

**Depends on:** "[confirmed] Four answers at the pause after attraction" (2026-09-14), (2); "[confirmed] The governor's seat" (2026-09-14); "[assumed] Attraction, built" (2026-09-14), (a) — the frozen seat, whose predicate this entry reuses; the residents' dividend it sits beside; D-6 (2026-09-15) — the pay is the first draw on the treasury's income that D-6's laws are to fund.

**[friday22 note]** (b) decides on the coordinator's word what happens to money accrued for a seat nobody sat in. "The tax was the city's" is a reading, not the principal's sentence; the alternative (escrow) is what a seat-holder returning from a halt might expect. Put it to him with (xlvii). `[assumed]`.

### 2026-09-15 — [confirmed] The transaction levy's shape: sales only, from the seller's receipt, off until a governor signs, split 30/30/30/10

Open (xlvi) closes. The four questions of the previous levy entry were put to the principal as options; his answers, verbatim as the options were put:

1. **Base** — **"Sales only: shop purchases, rent, wholesale, and cross-city sales as the receipt lands."** Over: every money movement, wages and the treasury's own payments included.
2. **Side** — **"Out of the seller's receipt."** Over: on top of the price, from the buyer.
3. **Split** — his own words: **"30 treasury/30 world devlopment/30 percent just in case fund investmnets / 10 percent i manage it first 10 years"**. Recorded as: **30 % to the city's treasury (governance), 30 % to a world development fund, 30 % to a reserve-and-investment fund ("just in case"), 10 % to a founder's account managed by the principal for the first ten years.** Over: half/half treasury and world fund; all to the treasury; all to the world fund.
4. **Default** — **"Off until a governor signs it."** Over: on from day one at 2 %. Consequence: every recorded hash stays put until a governor signs the levy.

**Chose:** `transactionLevy` is a levy on **sales only** — shop purchases, rent, wholesale, cross-city sales — taken **out of the seller's receipt** as it lands; **default 0, off until a governor signs it**; the take split **30 / 30 / 30 / 10** across treasury, world development fund, reserve-and-investment fund, founder's account.

**Over:** (1) every money movement — rejected, the levy sits on exchange, not on wages or the treasury's own flows. (2) buyer pays on top — rejected, the seller's receipt is the base. (3) half/half, all treasury, all world fund — rejected for the four-way split. (4) on from day one at 2 % — rejected; the levy is a governor's act, and no recorded world moves until one signs.

**Because:** in his words, the levy is the business model (previous entry: "the actual company … can invest and make money out of it"). D-6 — the governance makes money itself so it can govern. D-4 — one row a month, no row per act.

**Reversal cost:** **moderate before the first signed levy** — the field exists at 0 and no world has accrued a cent; the shape can move without touching a hash. **Expensive after** — four funds budget on it; every accumulated row would be wrong under a different base or side, and the split is what each fund has been told it will receive.

**Depends on:** "[confirmed] The 0.02 is a universal transaction levy, not a customs duty" (2026-09-15); "[confirmed] The governor's seat" (2026-09-14) — the levy is signed from that seat; "[confirmed] Four answers at the pause after attraction" (2026-09-14), the "one at a time" answer — this is the **third approved policy field**; D-4 (2026-09-14); D-6 (2026-09-15).

### 2026-09-15 — [assumed] The 2026-09-14 brief, the runtime and spec half: admission from the agent's side, the laws manifest, the MCP server
The principal's research brief (`docs/research/intake/2026-09-14-session-brief.md`) was acted on without touching the city lane. Rules the coordinator took, each on a builder's work and a guard's review; tagged for confirmation. The brief's own D-numbers are not used (Q-8).
**(a) Admission is specified from the agent's side, and the answer is honest.** `spec/admission.md` and the reference client `spec/tools/admit.py` (spec primitives only, never the runtime): for a hosted agent one controller signature, one POST, a signed record and a chain event back, no human, form or email on the path — the operator's bearer token is the operator's lock, not a step. An outside DID is refused `agent_unknown` until Q-3, closed rather than half-open. **Found and fixed:** the door appended `registry.admitted` without re-signing the passport, so the served pair withheld the admission until the agent's next activation; admit and revoke now refresh the passport under the same lock.
**(b) The laws are a signed, fetchable document.** `GET /.well-known/agent-port-laws.json`: the eight design rules verbatim with fixed ids, the mandate schema pinned by the SHA-256 of its exact bytes (the file rule, because an agent checks a downloaded file with `sha256sum`), the jurisdiction, the registry DID, the spec version, endpoints — signed by the registry key, 503 without one, never unsigned. Verifier tokens L1–L6 in `spec/laws-manifest.md`; validator 433 → 466. The one URL in the signed document comes from `AGENT_SOCIETY_PUBLIC_ORIGIN`, not the caller's Host header (jarvis11: an anonymous route plus a registry signature over a Host-derived URL is a signing oracle). **Found on the way:** a Windows clone checked the schema out with CRLF and the pinned hash changed; every file under `spec/` is now pinned to LF, because every one of them is hashed or byte-compared somewhere.
**(c) The MCP server never holds a key.** Six tools, stdio only, nothing listening: fetch the manifest, verify a passport / chain / admission record with the one verifier, build the *unsigned* admission request and the digest the controller must sign, submit a signed request to `<origin>/registry` after checking the controller signature locally, returning a refusal verbatim. No token it was not given; against a token-guarded door it returns the 401 as is.
**(d) The narrow novelty claim survives four rival papers read at source** (`docs/research/2026-09-14-rivals-verified.md`): no framework enforces a graded, domain-scoped economic-authority lattice checked per (domain, level) in code outside the model. Logic Monopoly's own future-work section names what our engine ships. The brief's striking numbers for that paper are its citations of other groups, not its results.
**Process, recorded as a lesson:** a commit takes the whole index — six fixture files another lane had staged rode into the P-1 commit and left HEAD failing its own validator for two commits; the fresh-clone check caught it and a second defect (the CRLF hash) in the same run.
**Not done here, by design:** P-4/P-5 (the settlement fee and its scenario) are Living World law and are the city lane's; P-6 (the reputation composite) waits on Q-3; npm/pip and ERC-8004 wait on a public remote and Q-4; engine pinning is Q-6.
**Verified:** runtime 583 passed; `run.sh` PASS at 466; a fresh clone at HEAD certifies.

### 2026-09-15 — [confirmed] City rent is levied too; the levy's band is free within its ceiling

**The questions put**, after jarvis11's read of the built levy. (1) **City rent.** The treasury owns most dwellings, so most rent is paid to the city. The coordinator's build had exempted it on the reading "the city taxing itself is circular". jarvis11 showed the reading wrong: under the 30/30/30/10 split only 30 % of a cut returns to the treasury; 70 % goes to the world development, reserve-and-investment and operations funds — so exempting city rent withholds the funds' share of the largest rent flow in the city. (2) **The band.** The record's earlier "downward only" (the previous levy entries) — a one-way ratchet, or free movement within the band?

**The principal's answers, verbatim as put.** (1) **"Yes — levy city rent too; the funds get their 70% of the largest rent flow."** (2) **"Free within the band, ceiling 0.02."**

**Chose:** (1) rent paid to the city's treasury is cut like any other sale receipt; the treasury's own 30 % returns to it and the 70 % goes to the three funds. (2) The band `[0, 0.02]` is a **ceiling**: within it the governor moves the rate freely, up or down. The record's earlier "downward only" now reads as **"the ceiling is 0.02; within the band the governor moves it freely"** — the previous entries' band line is superseded on this point, not deleted.

**Over:** (1) city rent stays exempt — rejected; the circularity argument holds only for the treasury's 30 %, not for the funds' 70 %. (2) a one-way ratchet (a rate, once lowered, may never rise) — rejected; "lower in the future" was the principal's forecast for the ceiling, not a rule binding each move.

**Because:** in his words — the funds "get their 70% of the largest rent flow"; "free within the band, ceiling 0.02".

**Reversal cost:** (1) cheap before any world signs the levy (default off, no hash moves); moderate after, since city-rent cuts will sit in the four funds' balances on the chain. (2) cheap — the band's check is one comparison; a ratchet would need the rate's history, which `policy:changed` rows already carry.

**Depends on:** "[confirmed] The transaction levy's shape: sales only, from the seller's receipt, off until a governor signs, split 30/30/30/10" (2026-09-15) — the split that makes the circularity reading wrong; "[confirmed] The 0.02 is a universal transaction levy" (2026-09-15) — whose "downward only" reading is narrowed here; "[assumed] The transaction levy, built" (next entry) — the city-rent seam is being added there per (1).

**[friday22 note]** The "downward only" line appeared in two earlier entries as the coordinator's reading `[assumed]`, never as the principal's word; this entry is the first time the band's direction was put to him. The (xlix) question — whether a governor may delegate the rate to a rule — is unchanged by this; a free band makes such a rule easier to write, not decided.
